<meta>This page begins the formal text of the study, following the Table of Contents. It introduces the Nuxalk people (historically referred to as the Bella Coola), their geographic location in British Columbia, their linguistic ties to the Salishan family, and the history of ethnographic research regarding their culture.</meta>

BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.

### I.

The <term>Bella Coola</term><note>Now known as the Nuxalk Nation.</note> are a small tribe inhabiting the coasts of Dean Inlet and Bentinck Arm, two long and narrow fiords situated at about latitude 52° north in British Columbia. Their territory extends along the Bella Coola River, which flows into the North Bentinck Arm. The name "Bella Coola" is a mispronunciation of the word *Bilxula*, which is the name used for the tribe by the <term>Kwakiutl</term><note>Now known as the Kwakwakaʼwakw.</note>. There is no single term in their own language that encompasses all the various groups who speak the Bella Coola language. It appears that in earlier times the tribe was quite numerous; however, due to various epidemics and the arrival of other diseases, their numbers have declined significantly, leaving only a few hundred people today.

The language spoken by the tribe belongs to the <term>Salishan family</term><note>A major linguistic group of the Pacific Northwest.</note>, specifically the group of dialects spoken along the coasts of Oregon, Washington, and British Columbia. The strong similarities between the Bella Coola language and other Coastal Salishan dialects lead me to believe that the tribes speaking these languages once lived in adjacent areas. Currently, the Bella Coola are separated from other Salishan-speaking tribes by a large stretch of land inhabited by tribes of <term>Athapascan</term><note>Linguistic family of the interior, including the Dene peoples.</note> and Kwakiutl descent. Their vocabulary and grammar have changed significantly over time, particularly through the frequent dropping of vowel sounds. The relationship of their language to other Coastal Salish dialects is similar to that of the <term>Tillamook</term><note>A Salishan-speaking group from the Oregon coast.</note> language, which is also spoken in an area separated from the rest of the Salishan territory by the region inhabited by the <term>Chinook</term> people.

Physically, the Bella Coola show signs of long-term contact with both the northern coastal tribes and the Athapascan tribes of the interior. Intermarriage appears to have been quite common—so much so that their physical appearance today is notably different from the southern Salishan tribes from whom they originated. The same is true for their customs and beliefs, which differ fundamentally from those of their southern Salishan relatives.

In this paper, I will describe the mythology of the Bella Coola and discuss how it developed.

### II.

Our previous knowledge of this tribe’s mythology is based on studies conducted by Adrian Jacobsen, Fillip Jacobsen, Goeken, and myself. In 1886, I published several brief notes on their traditions.¹ Goeken pub-

¹ *Proceedings of the Berlin Society for Anthropology, Ethnology, and Prehistory*, 1886, p. 206. <note>original: "Verhandlungen der Berliner Gesellschaft für Anthropologie, Ethnologie und Urgeschichte"</note>

<summary>This page introduces the Nuxalk (Bella Coola) people, detailing their geographic location in British Columbia and their linguistic classification as an isolated branch of the Salishan family. Boas notes that while they are linguistically related to southern tribes, their physical traits and culture have been heavily influenced by neighboring northern and interior groups.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Franz Boas’s introduction to Nuxalk (Bella Coola) culture, shifting from the history of the people to a critical review of earlier ethnographic research. ->BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.<-

I published some remarks on the religious life of the <term>Bella Coola</term><note>The Nuxalk people.</note> in 1886,¹ which, however, contain so many misconceptions that they cannot be used to advantage. At the same time I published a brief description of the tribe, based on information received from a small group of members of the tribe who were travelling in Germany. In this description also there are a number of misconceptions. The Indians were shown a collection of masks from <term>Vancouver Island</term><note>The large island off the coast of British Columbia, home to the Kwakwaka’wakw and Nuu-chah-nulth peoples.</note> with which they were not familiar. They gave, however, explanations of these masks, judging by the similarity to masks of their own tribe; consequently the explanations given there are not correct.² In 1890 I fell in with a number of Bella Coola who were fishing for salmon in <term>Fraser River</term><note>A major river in British Columbia, a vital site for salmon fishing and trade.</note>. The information obtained from them was published in the reports of the British Association for the Advancement of Science, 1891.³ A. Jacobsen published a description of their ceremonies in 1891.⁴ Fillip Jacobsen described some of their traditions and customs in 1894 and 1895.⁵ I published a collection of traditions in 1894 and 1895.⁶

### III.

All the collections which have been made heretofore do not bring out clearly the principal characteristic of the mythology of the Bella Coola. The tribes of the North Pacific coast consider the Sun as the most important deity, but at the same time they believe in a great many beings of supernatural power. For this reason their whole mythology is very unsystematic. The Bella Coola, on the other hand, have developed a peculiar mythology, in which a number of supernatural beings have been co-ordinated. A system has been evolved which justifies our terming the supernatural beings "deities." The general features of this system are as follows:—

The Bella Coola believe that there are five worlds, one above another. The middle one is our own world, the earth. Above it are spanned two heavens, while below it there are two underworlds. In the upper heaven resides the supreme deity, a woman who interferes comparatively little with the fates of mankind. In the centre of the lower heaven, that is in the <term>zenith</term><note>The highest point in the sky directly above an observer.</note>, stands the house of the gods, in which reside the Sun and all the other deities. Our own earth is an island swimming in the ocean. The underworld is inhabited by the ghosts, who are at liberty to return to heaven, whence they may be sent down again to our earth. The ghosts

<meta>This page continues the description of the Nuxalk cosmos, moving from the fate of the soul to the geography and inhabitants of the highest celestial realms. It details the supreme goddess Qamā'its and the structure of the two heavenly layers.</meta>

->BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.<-

Those who die a second death sink to the lowest world, from which there is no return.

The upper heaven is called <term>Atsa'axL</term><note>Literally "the top" or "the highest point."</note>, or *Snutx'lok'a'ls ti Sōnx t'aix'*, or *SōnxauL Omq'ō'mkilik'a*. The deity ruling there is a woman who is called **Qamā'its**, or *Tsi sisnāaxil* ("our woman"), or *Ek"!yak'imtōls'il* ("afraid of nothing").

This heaven is described as a prairie without any trees. It is said that in order to reach it one must go up the river from the house of the gods in the lower heaven. In another tradition it is said that in travelling from the lower heaven to the upper heaven one has to pass the rent in the sky which is called <term>Tslna'lōtas ti Sōnx t'aix'</term><note>The "rent" or "crack" in the sky acting as a portal between cosmic layers.</note>. The house of the supreme deity stands in the far east, and a gale is continually blowing over the open country, driving every thing towards the entrance of her house. Near the house, however, it is calm. In front of the house stands a post in the shape of a large winged monster, and its mouth is the entrance to the house. In front of the house-door there is gravel of three colors—blue, black, and white. Behind the house is a salt-water pond in which the goddess bathes. In this pond lives the **si'siul** or *xtsaltsalō'sem*<note>A powerful, double-headed supernatural serpent common in Northwest Coast mythology.</note>. This being sometimes descends to our world. Wherever it moves, the rocks burst, and slide down the sides of the mountains. It is described as a snake or a fish (see pp. 44, 66).

In the beginning of the world the mountains were of great height. They were human beings who made the world uninhabitable. **Qamā'its** made war upon the mountains. She vanquished them, and made them smaller than they used to be. During this fight she broke off the nose of the mountain **Yūlyule'ml**, which is situated near <term>Nul!e'l</term><note>A location in Nuxalk territory.</note>. Its face may be recognized even now; and the Indians say that when its name is called, it answers. There are two mountains near the head-waters of Bella Coola River. The one is called **Slex'lēkoai'l**; the other one, **Na'axl**. The former had a fire, called **Snutai'k'nimsta**, burning in his house. This fire warned him of the approach of enemies. When **Qamā'its** made war upon the mountains, the fire warned its master. **Qamā'its** was coming down the river in her canoe, which is named "**T!kun**." When she approached, he broke her canoe, and she returned to heaven. The canoe has been transformed into stone, and may be seen to this day at the foot of the mountain **Slex'lēkoai'l**. It is said that **Qamā'its** visits the earth every now and then. Her visits cause sickness and death. She is described as a great warrior.

In the centre of the lower heaven, which is called **Sōnx**, stands the house of the gods. This house is named **Nusme'ta** (the House of Myths), or *Nusk!oaltnai'x'sta* ("where man was created"), or *Nusk'lalne'msta* ("the house from which people come down"), or *Nusqulxoai'x'sta* ("the house to which people go"). In front of the house stands a post called **Nultnē'k'ta**. It is painted with representations of all kinds of birds. A

<meta>This page continues the description of the Nuxalk pantheon, shifting focus from the supreme goddess Qamā'its in the highest heaven to the deities of the lower heaven, specifically the Sun, who is the central figure of Nuxalk prayer and ritual.</meta>

->BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.<-
29

The white crane (?), <term>Qō'xōx</term><note>A supernatural bird figure sitting atop the celestial house.</note>, is sitting on top of the post. The master of the house is **SEnx**, the **Sun**. He is also called <term>Tā'ata</term><note>Literally "Our Father."</note>, or <term>Smai'yakila</term><note>Meaning "The Sacred One."</note>, or *Smayalō'lla*. It seems that he is the only deity to whom the Bella Coola <note>The historical name used for the Nuxalk people.</note> pray. They do not pray to **Qamā'its**, the deity of the supreme heaven. I have not found any prayers directed to the other deities of the lower heaven. I recorded a number of such prayers directed to the Sun. They are as follows:

> "Look on us where we are going, Father!" <note>original: "Alk·!x'īLō'Lsux Tāatau'!"</note>
> "Take care of us, Father!" <note>original: "Tāatau'! alk·altx'omdō'Lx!"</note>
> "Father! Take care of our road! Take care of us!" <note>original: "Tāatau'! lk·altxumtō'Lx ala mentalt'a'ts! Tāatau' alēp'alōsaltōLx!"</note>

After a long-continued rain, they pray:

> "Wipe your face, Father! that it may be fair weather." <note>original: "TspōsE'mx Tāatau'! kōL'iē'lxustimō'tx, Tāatau'!"</note>

The mountain-goat hunter prays:

> "Let your children look at me, SmayalōLla, Father!" <note>original: "Ōsqa yūstūtānx, Tāatau', ta menme'ntsnō, SmāyalōLlau'!"</note>

A person pursued by misfortune prays:

> "Make me happy, Father! You have given me too much misfortune." <note>original: "Nuqlamēk·i'mtsx Tāatau'! anuqomak·ma'tō ti q!ayanemnemt'ai'x'."</note>

A sick person prays:

> "Let my life be long, Father!" <note>original: "Nuqlamēk·i'mtsx Tāatau'! sk·a sasq!oalostō'mx Tāatau'!"</note>

The successful hunter, or the woman who has found a plentiful supply of berries, prays:

> "Father! You make me happy. You give me what I desire. Thus I find what I wished for." <note>original: "Nōnōqalamē'k·tuts ti māntLt'aix' sk·a nōqlamēk·imts sk·a pōLtus anoai'k·mēts'ats sk·ētsk·is ti sq!aitst'aix'."</note>

The Bella Coola also make offerings to the Sun. Hunters throw four small slices of seal-meat, of mountain-goat tallow, etc., into the fire as an offering to the Sun, in order to obtain success in hunting. Sick people burn parts of their clothing, which they decorate with red cedar-bark, as an offering to regain health.

The second deity, who is called **Alk'untā'm**, seems to be of equal importance. **SEnx** and **Alk'untā'm** stay in the rear of the House of Myths. Near the fire stays **SnūLk'ulx·ā'ls**. He is an old man who formerly ruled over

</margin>
the House of Myths, but who has given up his place in favor of <term>SENX</term><note>The Sun deity, the central figure of Nuxalk ritual.</note> and <term>ALK'UNTÁ'M</term><note>A supreme creator deity and lord of the celestial house.</note>. These two might be called the rulers of mankind. In most traditions they are described as trying to destroy man, notwithstanding the fact that they are considered the creators of mankind. This peculiar characteristic of these deities is clearly brought out in the traditions recorded on pages 78 and following. In one legend which I collected in 1890, ALK'UNTÁ'M's mother, <term>Nūnusōmik·ēeqone'm</term><note>A monstrous cannibalistic figure.</note>, is described as a Cannibal, who inserts her long snout in the ears of man, and sucks out his brain. Eventually she was transformed into the mosquito.¹ I did not hear her mentioned as one of the deities residing in the House of Myths. According to the same tradition, the salmon were obtained by a man who gambled with ALK'UNTÁ'M, the stake being the salmon. ALK'UNTÁ'M lost, and the man took the salmon down from heaven. Another legend of the origin of the salmon is recorded on page 38, where it is told how the birds obtained the salmon. According to still another tradition (page 94), the Raven obtained the salmon by marrying a salmon girl. In the tradition quoted above, ALK'UNTÁ'M also gives to man the power to cure disease by means of the water of life, which the shaman sprinkles on the sick person.

A number of inferior deities live in the House of Myths. They might be called the assistants of the principal deities. In order to understand their functions, it is necessary to state that the deities residing in the House of Myths have particular charge of the religious winter ceremonial of the Bella Coola <note>The Nuxalk people.</note> which is called <term>kū'siut</term><note>The sacred winter dance season, a cornerstone of Nuxalk social and spiritual life.</note>, and which corresponds to the <term>ts'etsá'eqa</term><note>The Kwakwaka'wakw (Kwakiutl) 'Cedar Bark Dance' or winter ceremonial.</note> of the Kwakiutl. I have described this ceremonial, and its importance in the social life of the Kwakiutl, in another paper.² The *kū'siut* is of equal importance to an understanding of the social life and mythology of the Bella Coola. It is sufficient to say at this place that the ceremonials performed during the *kū'siut* are mostly dramatic representations of myths referring to the various deities, particularly to those of the House of Myths: therefore masks representing these deities are used in the ceremonials. Plates VII to XII³ show a series of these masks. Figures 1, 2, and 4 (Plate VII) are SENX, ALK'UNTÁ'M, and <term>SNŪLK'ULX·Ā'LS</term><note>A significant deity of the House of Myths often depicted in masks.</note>. The functions of many of the inferior deities seem to refer solely to the *kū'siut*. These deities and their functions are as follows: <term>Six·sēk·ilai'x·</term><note>A deity who ordains death; the name implies a 'finisher' or 'ender'.</note> (Plate VII, Figure 6) ordains the death of man and animals. According to one statement that I received, there are four deities of this name in the House of Myths. It is his particular duty to kill those who transgress the laws of the *kū'siut*. This deity is mentioned by Jacobsen, who calls him Sek-seik Kallai.⁴ <term>Nusnē'neq'als</term><note>A supernatural being associated with the forest giants.</note> (Plate VII, Figure 7), or the <term>Snēnē'iq</term><note>A class of forest monsters or giants in Nuxalk folklore.</note> of the House of Myths, sits by himself in one

corner of the house. He prevents those who are not initiated in the secrets of the <term>kū'siut</term><note>A sacred Nuxalk winter ceremonial and secret society.</note> from approaching the house. The <term>Snēnē'iq</term><note>A monstrous supernatural being often featuring prominently in Nuxalk masks and folklore.</note> is a fabulous monster, the peculiarities of which are desc<unclear>rib</unclear>ed in a number of traditions (see pp. 83 ff.). <term>S'anōLx'mula'lt</term> (Plate VII, Figs. 12 and 13) is a boy who performs <term>kū'siut</term> dances all the time. When the deities resolve to send a new dance down to our world, it is conveyed by <term>S'anōLx'mula'lt</term>. It is the office of another deity to sing, accompanying the <term>kū'siut</term> dances of the gods (Plate VII, Fig. 3). <term>Anuqat'ōtsEm</term> is a deity in regard to whose functions I have not been able to obtain detailed information. It is said that he intervenes on behalf of man when <term>SEnx</term><note>The Sun deity.</note> and <term>Alk'untā'm</term><note>The supreme creator deity.</note> threaten to punish him. There are two goddesses in the house who also intervene in favor of man when the principal deities threaten him with death and sickness. Their names are <term>Snitsma'na</term> (Plate VII, Fig. 9) and <term>AiaLilā'axa</term> (Plate VII, Figs. 10 and 11). They wake man after sleep. Without their help, nobody could wake from sleep. <term>AiaLilā'axa</term> is at the same time the guardian of the moon. Every month she restores the moon to her full size, and she cleans her face after an eclipse. The moon is called <term>A'nL'āLg·ila</term> (Plate X, Fig. 10). The eclipse is produced by several deities called <term>Aiq'on'yosnEm</term>, which means "painting the face black" (Plate X, Figs. 4-6). It is believed that at this time the moon performs one of the most sacred ceremonies of the <term>kū'siut</term>, which are thought to be very dangerous to the performer. The black paint with which her face is covered is supposed to be a protection against these dangers. <term>AiaLilā'axa</term> cleans off the paint after the dance has been completed. According to Jacobsen,¹ the Indians say that during an eclipse the moon (<term>En-kla-loi'-killa</term><note>A variant phonetical transcription of the Nuxalk word for the moon.</note>) paints her face black. The same deities are believed to paint and to clean the faces of human <term>kū'siut</term> dancers. <term>Snitsma'na</term> and <term>AiaLilā'axa</term> also resuscitate those who are killed by the dangerous performances of the <term>kū'siut</term>.

While the functions of the beings enumerated here are mostly concerned with the <term>kū'siut</term>, ot<unclear>h</unclear>ers are more immediately concerned with the affairs of the world. Important among these is <term>K·x·ēx·ēk·nē'm</term> (Plate VII, Fig. 5). <term>SEnx</term> is the creator of man, but his work is supplemented by that of the god <term>K·x·ēx·ēk·nē'm</term>. When <term>SEnx</term> creates a new-born child, <term>K·x·ēx·ēk·nē'm</term> gives the child its individual features. Before children are born, the goddess named <term>Nuēx·qEmalsai'x·</term> or <term>Sēmsēme'ltstas SEnxalā'olEla</term> places them in a cradle and rocks them. After she ceases rocking them, the children are sent down to our world. She also rocks the young of all animals; and when she stops, <term>SEnx</term> sends them down to our wor<unclear>l</unclear>d to be born. At the same time he ordains that their skins and their flesh shall serve for clothing and food for man. <term>Nuēx·qEmalsai'x·</term> might therefore be called the deity

...having charge of the birth of all animal beings. While she is rocking the cradle, she sings,—

> ->"I am rocking the one who is born; he shall grow up. Au, au, au, au.<-
> ->I am making him grow, I am making him strong, following the path of his ancestors. Au, au, au, au."<-
<note>Original: "xoëxoësmaix'nimōta nēk'ō'k's La nōëx'L!E'malsaix' au au au au. Aayotsētsk'ōx'ats six'ixōlayā'mk'tēts'ats snemnemk'a'ltowasōats ayawasōyōnxsōats au au au au." This ritual song is intended to foster the growth and vitality of all living things.</note>

Another deity living in the House of Myths is the mother of flowers, called <term>Nono'osqa</term> (Plate X, Figs. 7 and 9), the daughter of <term>Snukpa'nlits</term>. It seems that this last name means "going to the right." This evidently refers to the fact that the Sun is believed to move on his path towards the west, face forward, and consequently during the spring months ascends the sky moving towards the right; so that <term>Snukpa'nlits</term>, moving to the right, means at the same time the springtime, when the sun is moving up the sky. This is the time when flowers begin to sprout. Every spring <term>Nono'osqa</term> gives birth to all the plants in the order in which they begin to appear. A shaman is called to her aid by two old women who assist her (Plate X, Figs. 8 and 11).

Every year, at the time of the winter solstice, the deities determine who shall die during the ensuing year. Two beings called <term>Kakēstsai'ōL ōla xma'noas</term><note>Translates roughly to "those who swing on a plank."</note> are placed on the ends of a long plank, which is supported at its centre, and swings like a seesaw. Then all the men and all the animals are called to stand near the ends of the plank. When one of the swinging beings falls down from the plank, the person standing near it will die sooner than the one standing at the opposite end. The deities have a messenger called <term>NutsEkoa'lsika</term> (that means "long ear"), who carries all the news from our earth up to the House of Myths. <term>Alk'untā'm</term><note>The supreme creator deity.</note> has two children,—the deer, who is called <term>Snoō'lExELts ALk'untā'm</term>, that is, "the foolish son of <term>Alk'untā'm</term>" (Plate VII, Fig. 8), and <term>LēExonix'is ALk'untā'm</term>. I have not been able to learn anything in regard to the functions of these two beings.

While <term>SEnx</term><note>The Sun deity.</note> and <term>Alk'untā'm</term> are principally concerned with the fates of mankind, they do not personally interfere with the doings of man. Their thoughts are carried out by four brothers, who are called collectively <term>Masmasala'nix</term> or <term>Ontsk'ē'mtEnEm</term> (Plate VIII, Figs. 1-4). These brothers are <term>MaLapā'litsēk</term> ("the one who finishes his work by chopping once"), <term>Yula'timot</term> ("the one who finishes his work by rubbing once"), <term>MaL'apē'exoēk</term> ("the one who finishes his work by cutting once"), and <term>IL'iLu'lak</term>. They have a sister called <term>L'a'qumēiks</term>. These four brothers live in an elevated room in the rear of the House of Myths. They are engaged in carving and painting. It is said that they gave man his arts. They taught him to build canoes, to make boxes, to build houses, and to carve in wood and to

The Bella Coola <note>The Nuxalk people of the Pacific Northwest.</note> say, when carving a design, that <term>Masmasala'nix</term><note>A group of four brothers who are the master craftsmen and architects of the divine realm.</note> gives them the idea which they are working out.

<term>SENX</term><note>The Sun deity and supreme being.</note> has a daughter named <term>Sp'ix'p'ik'nē'm</term>, who invented the art of working cedar-bark. Her figure is also used in the <term>kū'siut</term><note>The sacred winter ceremonial or "secret" dance society.</note> ceremonial, in which the invention of the preparation of cedar-bark is represented. This deity has an assistant called <term>Omatōsē'k'</term>, who supports the stick over the edge of which she is breaking the bark. When first breaking bark, she shouts, “Bring to me <term>Omatōsē'k'</term>” <note>original: “Alētx siāya'ltxau ti Omatōsē'k'stix”</note>. After he has been given to her, she shouts, “Bring me the board on which to break the bark” <note>original: “Alētx siāya'ltxau ta tqenk'Lts”</note>. After receiving this, she demands the cedar-bark breaker, saying, “Bring me the cedar-bark breaker” <note>original: “Alētx snukpanlai'ts ti sp'ē'k'tats”</note>. Finally she asks for cedar-bark, saying, “Bring me the cedar-bark” <note>original: “Alētx k"Lōllōlemx'laix' ti sp'ē'k'etstēx'”</note>. Then she begins to work, singing,—

> ->“Behold me, ye who are not initiated!<-
> ->I am the Cedar-bark Breaker of the Sun-place.”<-
> <note>original: “Ē sta alk'x'auwē't ōl'ensl'e msta la lau's / Ē sta ntsanē'ts tsi Sp'ix'p'ik'inē'mtas Senxalā'olla.” The "Sun-place" (Senxalā'olla) refers to the House of Myths where the Sun resides.</note>

It seems that most of the Bella Coola maintain that the Raven also lives in the House of Myths, but this point of their mythology is somewhat obscure and contradictory. According to the tradition of a number of families, the Raven was one of the beings sent down by <term>SENX</term> to our world to become the ancestor of man, but at the same time it is told that he invented certain arts. It is stated that he made the first salmon-trap (<term>altk'u'l</term>), which is used in connection with the salmon-weir.

It is also said that <term>Masmasala'nix</term> attempted to make the whistles for the winter ceremonial, that he was unsuccessful, and that the Raven succeeded in making them; also that the Raven came down to give the world its present shape. He instituted the festivals of the Bella Coola, and then returned to the House of Myths.

Besides all these deities, there are nine brothers and their sister particularly concerned with the observations of the <term>kū'siut</term> ceremonial (Plate IX, Figs. 1–9). The names of the brothers are, <term>Xēmxēmala'olla</term>, <term>Xē'mtsiwa</term>, <term>Omq'omki'lik'a</term>, <term>Q'o'mtsiwa</term>, <term>Aimalā'olela</term>, <term>Ai'umki'lik'a</term>, <term>Kulē'lias</term>, <term>Q'ulaxā'wa</term>, <term>A't'māku</term>; and their sister, <term>L'ētsāplēlāna</term>. These deities are painted with certain designs. <term>Xēmxēmala'olla</term> and <term>Xē'mtsiwa</term> are painted with the designs of the full moon (the former carries a staff wound with red and white cedar-bark); <term>Omq'omki'lik'a</term> and <term>Q'o'mtsiwa</term>, with the design of the half-moon; <term>Aimalā'olela</term> and <term>Ai'umki'lik'a</term>, with the design representing the stars; <term>Kulē'lias</term>, with the design of the rainbow; <term>Q'ulaxā'wa</term>, with the de-

She wears rings of red and white cedar-bark. The carving representing the kingfisher has the wings attached to the sides of the head, while the tail rises over the forehead. <term>Xēmxēmalā'ōLla</term> is said to carry a small woman in his arms. Her name is <term>Nuspō'xta</term>. When the brothers and their sister threaten to do harm to man, she entreats them to desist. In some traditions these deities are described as the children of <term>ALk'untā'm</term><note>The supreme creator deity of the Nuxalk, often associated with the Sun.</note>.^1

In one tradition <term>L'ētsa'aplēLāna</term> is described as visiting houses and stealing provisions. She is then pursued by the person whom she has wronged, and returns to the <term>House of Myths</term><note>The celestial residence of the gods in Nuxalk cosmology.</note>. The deities are unable to cure her; but the person who wounded her is called in, and withdraws his arrow, whereupon she recovers.^1 Formerly I had the impression that these ten deities were particularly concerned with the <term>sisau'k</term><note>A high-ranking ceremonial society associated with family crests and potlatching.</note> ceremonial,^2 but this impression has not been substantiated by the information I received during the past year. The ten deities appear much rather as deities of the <term>kū'siut</term><note>The sacred winter ceremonial, or "secret" dance society.</note>.

In the rear of the House of Myths there is a room named <term>LLō'sta</term>, in which the son of the deities lives. His name is <term>Ba'ExōLla</term> or <term>SnupaaxōLa'lt</term>. When <term>SENx</term><note>The Sun deity.</note> and <term>ALk'untā'm</term> desire to destroy their visitors, they send them past the door of <term>Ba'ExōLla</term>'s room. The latter then rushes out of his room and devours the visitors. He also initiates the <term>Cannibal</term><note>Known as the Hamatsa in other coastal cultures, this refers to a dancer initiated into a high-status secret society who performs a ritualized, ecstatic frenzy.</note>. According to the tradition of the tribe <term>SE'NXLEMX</term>, they acquired membership in the Cannibal Society in the following manner: <term>Ba'ExōLla</term> came down to the mountain <term>SqtsL</term>, where he met with the son of <term>SENXalō'Lela</term>, the first of the tribe. He conducted him up to the House of Myths. He took him into his room, and gave him the name <term>Q'oalaiu'tstimot</term>. He put a snake into his body, which enabled him to pass through the water. When the youth applied his mouth to the body of a person, the snake tore pieces of flesh from the body, and devoured it. Then <term>Ba'ExōLla</term> took the youth to the upper heaven, past the rent in the sky, and to the house of the supreme deity, <term>Qama'its</term><note>A powerful female deity who resides in the highest level of the spirit world.</note>. The two approached the house, being blown towards it by the strong gale prevailing in the open country of the upper heaven. They found <term>Qama'its</term> sitting in front of her house; and she said to <term>Ba'ExōLla</term>, “Why don’t you come in? You wish that your friend should obtain great supernatural power. Bring him to my house, and I will give him what you desire. Stay for a short while where you are, and I will show you what I am doing. Watch closely when the post of my house closes its eyes.” After a little while the post closed its eyes. It grew dark at once, and the two visitors fainted, but soon they recovered. When the post opened its eyes again, it grew light. The visitors remained sitting on the ground, and

# BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.

Suddenly a strong wind began to blow, which rolled them over the prairie until they reached the door of the house. Then suddenly the wind calmed down. They remained sitting on the ground near the doorway; and <term>Qamā'its</term><note>The supreme female deity of the Nuxalk people, who resides in the highest level of heaven and is the most powerful of the supernatural beings.</note> said, "Watch closely when the post of my house closes its eyes." They were sitting opposite each other, watching the post; and when it closed its eyes, they were transformed into two stones, but they soon regained human shape. Then <term>Qamā'its</term> asked them to enter. Now the woman took the youth's blanket, and gave him another one made of bear-skin set with fringes of red cedar-bark. She told him that this blanket was to keep him warm, and that it would direct his course. Next she fetched some water from the salt-water pond behind her house. She sprinkled it over the faces of her visitors, and told the youth to sing about his experiences in the upper heaven when performing the <term>Cannibal dance</term><note>Part of the Hamatsa or similar secret society rituals, where the initiate acts out the character of a man-eating spirit to eventually be tamed by the community.</note>. If she had not sprinkled the faces of her visitors with water, they would have died. She said to the youth, "Your country is not far away. Do not be afraid of the dangerous road that you have to pass. Later on there shall be many Cannibals like you. Do not be afraid to touch the food that another Cannibal may offer to you. You are strong because you have seen me." Then <term>Qamā'its</term> sent him back to the lower sky. Here the gods placed him on the back of a bird, <term>Sqᵘxsᴇɴ</term><note>A supernatural bird that acts as a carrier between the celestial world and the earth.</note>, which carried him down to the sea. As soon as the bird reached the water, it uttered its cry, and at the same time the young man uttered the cry of the Cannibal. The people heard it, and said to one another, "That must be the boy whom we lost some time ago." They connected many canoes by means of planks, and paddled out to the place where the bird was swimming about. They covered the canoes with red cedar-bark and eagle-down, and tried to capture the youth; but when they approached, the bird swam towards the village. They surrounded it with their canoes; but the bird flew up, and disappeared in the sky, and at the same time the youth flew towards the village. When the people landed, he attacked them, taking hold of their arms; and the snake, which was still in his body, tore pieces of flesh out of their arms. The people sang and beat time in order to appease him.

In a second room in the rear of the <term>House of Myths</term><note>The celestial residence of the Nuxalk deities.</note>, next to that of the Cannibal, lives <term>Kōkō'sʟxᴇᴍ</term><note>A minor deity who assists in the initiation rituals of secret societies.</note>, another son of the deities. His room is called <term>Nus'ō'lxsta</term><note>A specific ceremonial chamber within the heavenly house.</note>. He initiates the <term>Olx-dancer</term><note>A specific rank or role within the Nuxalk ceremonial winter dances.</note> (see Chap. VI).

The path of the Sun is guarded by a number of deities. At sunrise is stationed the <term>Bear of Heaven</term>, <term>Sna̠nō's ti Sōnx· t'aix·</term><note>The Nuxalk name literally translates as 'The Bear of the Sun'.</note>. <note>This illustration shows the Bear of Heaven, depicted as a fierce guardian with warrior attributes.</note> (Plate X, Fig. 2). He is described as a fierce warrior, who protects the Sun against the attacks of his enemies, and he is the cause of the warlike spirit of man. His hair is tied up in a knot on top of his head. His mask is used in the <term>sisau'k·</term><note>A high-ranking ceremonial society whose members display family crests through masks and dances.</note>

ceremonial, and sometimes in the *kūs'siut* <note>A secret society of the Nuxalk people, also known as the "Initiates," whose rituals involve complex masked dances during the winter.</note>. The following song belongs to his mask and dance:—

->“Cry now, as though you had left me! I shall tie up my hair, warriors, like the Bear of Heaven.” <note>original Nuxalk: "Nanēmō'tstxuit ti q'oyaki'mtst'aix'. Nō'noq'awēxum ts'ēn t'ayōLelat'aix' ayati snanō's ti sōnx' t'aix', wā, nān, ai!"</note><-

At sunset stands an enormous post which is called *The Post that Supports the Sun* <note>original Nuxalk: Nutēexoā'axtatas ti Sōnx' t'aix'.</note>. It supports the sky, and prevents the Sun from falling down into the lower world. The trail of the Sun is described as a bridge which was built by <term>Masmasalā'nix</term><note>One of the four creator deities who reside in the House of Myths and shaped the world.</note>. The bridge is as broad as the distance between the winter solstice and the summer solstice. The Sun walks, his face turned towards the west. In summer he walks on the right-hand side of the bridge, in winter on the left-hand side of the bridge, which explains the varying heights of the sun in the course of the year. The extreme right and extreme left of the bridge are called *The Place Where He Sits Down* <note>original Nuxalk: Sē'Emt</note> (that is, the solstices). At each of these points a being is placed who is called <term>Alk'x'ē'Lnem</term> <note>Refer to Plate X, Fig. 3 for an illustration of this solstice guardian.</note>. It is their duty to see that the Sun does not tarry too long at the solstice. If in summer or winter he should be inclined to stay too long or to return too soon, they regulate his course. When the Sun tarries too long at the winter solstice, the people say, "*salmon will be dried late this year*" <note>original Nuxalk: “ēx tsēs pa'nia”</note>. If he leaves it without tarrying, they say, "*soon we shall dry salmon*" <note>original Nuxalk: “ēk'!x' tsēs pa'nia”</note>. Three guardians named <term>Naqumiqa'otsaix</term> <note>Refer to Plate X, Fig. 1 for an illustration of these spirits.</note> accompany the Sun on his course, dancing around him all the time. The halo is called *the cape of our father* <note>original Nuxalk: Itwu'xtsia ti Mānl t'aix'</note>. A sun-dog <note>A parhelion, or bright spot in the sky that looks like a mock sun.</note> that appears westward from the Sun is called *the painted face of our father* <note>original Nuxalk: ALqōl ti Mānl t'aix'</note>. The <term>Bella Coola</term><note>The Nuxalk people of the central coast of British Columbia.</note> believe that when it drops down to our earth, it causes epidemics. During an eclipse the Sun is believed to lose his torch. The rays of the Sun are his eyelashes.

There are twenty-four guardians appointed to take care of the sky. They are called <term>Nexolak'ai'x</term>. According to tradition, the sky must be continually fed with firewood. Once upon a time they put too much firewood into the sky and made it burst. All the pieces except one, called <term>S'ALwalō'sEM</term>, fell down to our earth. The fragments hit the faces of the twenty-four guardians, and distorted them. They tried to mend the sky, but did not know how to do it. They went down the river, and came to <term>Masmasalā'nix</term>, whose assistance they asked. <term>Masmasalā'nix</term> gathered up the broken pieces, and glued them together. Up to that time the Sun had stayed in the east, but now he began to go on his daily course. At that time <term>Masmasalā'nix</term> built the bridge over which the Sun travels every day. He placed a wedge in the opening of the sky, into which the <term>Nexolak'ai'x</term>...

<summary>This page describes the Nuxalk understanding of the sun's path across a celestial bridge and the supernatural guardians who regulate the seasons and the sky. It also recounts a myth where the creator deity Masmasalā'nix mends the sky after it burst from excessive firewood.</summary>

This opening is called <term>K·awa'umsta</term> <note>Nuxalk: *K·awa'umsta*, literally translated as "mouth kept open by means of a wedge."</note>. <term>Masmasala'nix</term> <note>One of the four creator deities who shaped the world.</note> spoke: “The sky shall not burst again. This wedge shall keep its mouth open.” The following <term>*ku'siut*</term> <note>A sacred winter ceremonial society of the Nuxalk people.</note> song refers to these deities:—

> My child perished like the sky when it broke. <note>original Nuxalk: Ai'mats tā mnatsai' tūsxts ti sō'nx'tsgi t'aix'.</note>
> Go to <term>S'alwalō'sEm</term> <note>A celestial location or being associated with the upper world.</note> of the sky, my child! <note>original Nuxalk: AiēlX tā mnatsai' ōL ti S'alwalō'sEms ti Sōnx' t'aix'.</note>
> Gladden my heart, my child! <note>original Nuxalk: Sk'a ya'lx'tux ti si'lxnō tā mnatsai'.</note>
> Sit down in the mouth of the sky, my child! <note>original Nuxalk: Sk'a anōg'a wa EXEmē'x ti K'awa'umstask' ti Sōnx' t'aix' tā mnatsai'.</note>

Our world is called **A'nēkō'ōL** or **QEnk'i'lst**, that is, “the land below.” It is an island swimming in the boundless ocean. In the far east a giant is sitting with legs apart, who is called <term>Alēp!alaxtnaix'</term>. He holds a long stone bar in his outstretched hands. The earth is fastened to this stone bar by means of two stone ropes. Sometimes he gets tired, and moves his hands to take better hold of the stone bar. Then we have an earthquake; and the Nuxalk <note>Boas uses the historical term "Bella Coola."</note> say, “<term>Snēnik<sup>u</sup>pstak'imtōLs</term>,” that is, “he takes hold of our world.” When he moves our earth westward, we have epidemics. When he moves it eastward, all sickness disappears.

In the ocean lives a being called <term>SēLsāts</term>, who twice every day swallows the water of the sea and gives it forth again. This is the cause of the tides. A mask representing this being appears in the *ku'siut* ceremonial. He is represented as a human being, the face of which is painted with white stripes, which symbolize the various levels of the sea.

The world below us is the country of the ghosts (<term>*kōLk<sup>u</sup>LōLe'mx'*</term>). It is called **Asiutā'nEm**. Descriptions of the ghosts' country are principally obtained from shamans who believe they have visited that country during a trance. According to the statement of an old woman who believed that as a little girl she had visited the country of the ghosts during a trance, the entrance to the country of the ghosts is through a hole situated in each house, between the doorway and the fireplace. The country of the ghosts stretches along the sandy banks of a large river. There is a hill behind their village, the base of which is covered with sharp stones. When it is summer here, it is winter there. When it is night here, it is day there. The ghosts do not walk on their feet, but on their heads. Their language is different from the one spoken on earth. The souls, on reaching the lower world, receive new names. The village of the ghosts is said to be surrounded by a fence. They have a dancing-house, in which they perform

<summary>This page describes Nuxalk cosmology, including the "mouth of the sky," a giant who holds the earth in place and causes earthquakes, the origin of tides, and the inverted nature of the land of the ghosts.</summary>

# BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.

### 38

their **kū'siut** <note>The sacred winter ceremonial society of the Nuxalk people; here, the ghosts are said to perform their own version of these dances.</note>. It is just below the burial-place of each village. The dancing-house is very large and long. It has four fires. The women stay on the floor of the house, while the men sit on an elevated platform. The houses have doors, but the ghosts who first reach the lower world enter the house through the smoke-hole. A rope ladder placed in the smoke-hole facilitates their entrance. Two men stand at the foot of the ladder. They are called <term>Anōel'axsalai'x'</term> <note>Guardian figures in the underworld who receive the spirits of the recently deceased.</note>. For a person who has once entered the dancing-house there is no return to our earth. The souls are at liberty to return to the lower heaven, which they reach by ascending the rope ladder. Those who return to the lower heaven are sent back to our earth by the deities, to be born as children in the same family to which they belonged. Those who enjoy life in the country of the ghosts, and who do not return to heaven, die a second death, and then sink to the second lower world, from which there is no return.

I received another description from an old man. He stated that he reached the country in his canoe. He saw two trails,—one the trail of the living, one that of the dead. He followed the trail of the dead, and reached a village in which there was a dancing-house. The language of the ghosts differed from that of the living (see p. 42).

The Nuxalk <note>Boas uses the historical term "Bella Coola."</note> believe that in the far west is situated the land of the salmon, which is called <term>Mia'ltoa</term> <note>The mythical country where salmon live in human form before migrating to the rivers of the world.</note>. The salmon leave this country early in the spring every year, and ascend the rivers. They are believed to return to their own country in the fall. The following tradition is of importance, explaining the manner in which the salmon were first brought from their country to the rivers of our world:—

Once upon a time a man named <term>Winwī'na</term> <note>A name meaning "The Warrior" or "The One who gathers wealth through strife."</note> lived at <term>Q'ō'mqūtis</term> <note>A major Nuxalk village formerly located on the north bank of the mouth of the Bella Coola River.</note>. One day he was sitting in front of his house, looking at the river. He thought, "I wish fish would ascend this river." At that time not a single salmon visited Bella Coola River. <term>Winwī'na</term> entered his house and lay down, thinking about the salmon. One night while he was asleep he dreamt that with the help of all the animals he had made war upon the salmon, that he had vanquished them, and that since that time the salmon had ascended Bella Coola River. When he awoke he invited all the animals to his house, and told them about his dream. They all came, and when they had entered he shut the door. Then he spoke: "My brothers, I have invited you to my house that you may hear what I wish to do. You shall help me to obtain what I desire." The Mink asked him in what they were to assist him; and he replied, "I want to go to <term>Mia'ltoa</term>. There is not a single fish in our river, and I dreamt that with your help I vanquished the fish. Let us make war upon them. I shall certainly take some slaves, and we will place them in this river." Mink retorted, "I am glad that you are speaking

## BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.

### 39

in regard to this matter. I asked my father the Sun <note>In Nuxalk cosmology, the Sun (Atquā'ntam) is the supreme deity and the father of the supernatural beings who descended to earth.</note> (see Chap. V) to give us salmon, and I think he gave you the dream which you told us."

All the birds agreed, and they resolved to start as soon as possible. Then **Winwi'na** <note>A supernatural leader whose name is associated with the concept of "making war" or leading an expedition.</note> asked **Masmasala'nix** <note>One of the four celestial master carpenters or creators who fashioned the first humans and their tools.</note> to build a canoe. The latter complied with his request, and made a self-moving canoe, to which he gave the names “Winaiótstuls” and “Kunkunu'qtstuls.” In the third moon after the winter solstice the canoe was completed, and **Winwi'na** started, accompanied by the clouds, the birds, and by all the animals. The **Hermaphrodite** <note>A recurring character in Nuxalk myth who often occupies a unique social and spiritual role; in this story, they occupy the stern, a position of steering or significance.</note> was sitting in the stern of the canoe. They went down the fiord; and when they passed the village of **Bella Bella** <note>The territory of the Heiltsuk people, neighbors to the Nuxalk.</note>, they saw the **Cormorant** sitting on the beach, who asked to be taken along as a passenger. They travelled westward for a long time, and finally they reached the country of the **Salmon**. They saw that there were no trees. The country was a vast prairie. A large sun was shining in the sky. Soon they descried the village of the **Salmon**. They sent out the **Raven** as a spy. When he returned, he told them that in the evening the **Salmon** were in the habit of playing on the beach. **Mink** suggested that this would be the best time for carrying some of them away. Then the **Crane** (according to another version, the **Hawk**) said, “I shall carry away the **Sockeye Salmon**.” The **Wren** said, “I shall carry away the **Humpback Salmon** <note>Also known as Pink Salmon.</note>.” The **Kingfisher** (according to another version, the **Crane**) said, “I shall carry away the **Dog Salmon** <note>Also known as Chum Salmon.</note>.” The **Raven** said, “I shall carry away the **Silver Salmon** <note>Also known as Coho Salmon.</note>.” The **Fish-Hawk** said, “I shall carry away the **Olachen** <term>Olachen</term> <note>Also spelled eulachon; a small, oily smelt-like fish prized for its oil, often called 'candlefish.'</note> and the **Salmon Trout**.” The **Cloud** said, “I shall carry away the **Spring Salmon** <note>Also known as Chinook Salmon.</note>.” Finally the **Cormorant** said, “I am only a passenger, and I will take whatever I can get.” The **Mink** remarked, “I will not say what I am going to carry away : I only want to tell you that you must each take one male and one female. Now start. You are invisible to the **Salmon**. When you approach them, they will not be able to see you, just as we cannot see the ghosts, even when they are walking by our side.” They left **Winwi'na** to guard the canoe. Then all the birds and the **Mink** took each one male and one female child of the various kinds of fish. When they carried them off, the children fainted, as though their souls had been taken away. Their bodies remained at the place where they had been playing. The **Salmon** did not see their captors, and did not know why the children were fainting. The birds returned to the canoe, carrying the fish. Then **Winwi'na** said, “Let us go on and see what is beyond the country of the **Salmon**.” Soon they arrived at a place called **Qoalē'nia** (this name is not quite certain), in which vast numbers of berries were growing. Here the **Hermaphrodite** went ashore, and picked all kinds of berries, which she carried into the canoe. Then they returned home. For seven months they had staid in the country of the **Salmon**. They reached the coast shortly after the winter solstice (?). When they passed **Bella Bella**, the **Cormorant**...

[The Raven] said, "This is my home. I will go ashore here." He went, and took along the Salmon which he had captured. Ever since that time there are salmon at **Bella Bella** <note>The Heiltsuk (Bella Bella) territory, where the Raven is a central figure in mythology.</note>. The others travelled on, and came to the mouth of **Bella Coola River**. Then they threw all the various kinds of fish into the water. The Salmon jumped, and began to ascend the river. Then **Winwi'na** <note>The leader of the expedition; his name is associated with the arrival of resources.</note> arose in his canoe and told each at what season he was to arrive. He scattered the berries over the mountains and through the valleys, and told them at what season to ripen. After he had done so, he invited his companions into his house, and gave them a feast.

In this tradition the birds and animals are not called by their ordinary names, but by mythical names. These names are as follows: —

According to the belief of the Bella Coola, **Winwi'na's** canoe arrives from the country of the Salmon every year. It stays in the country of the Bella Coola for nine months, and then returns to the country of the Salmon. At the moment when it leaves, another canoe, which is named "**Nō'ak˙nem**" <note>The vessel that brings the winter spirits.</note> or "**Nunuk·au'tsnem**," which brings the ***kū'siut*** <note>The second most important secret society ceremonial, traditionally held in the winter months.</note> ceremonial, arrives from the country **S'anōk˙!pta'ltua**. The canoe reaches a distant point of land before the departure of the canoe of the Salmon. After four days it reaches a nearer point of land. Four days more, and it is seen at the point of land nearest to the mouth of Bella Coola River. Another four days, and it reaches the mouth of the river. 

The Indians believe that there is a house named **Snō'amLtenank˙** at this place. A post is standing in the water in front of the house. It is called **Snūtexoalāaxtstenā'nk˙**. In the house live three men who are named **NaapsuLaaxai'x·**, **A'mLtag'ilis**, and **Tix·ti·k˙'ā'nemēm**. The canoe is tied up in front of this house. As soon as it arrives, the *kū'siut* ceremonial begins. At the head-waters of Bella Coola River, forming the watershed between Bute Inlet and Bentinck Arm, is a mountain called

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.</header>

**Smaya'na** <note>A significant mountain near the Bella Coola River, personified as a supernatural being.</note> is considered to be a human being. It is said that his children make the canoe travel up the **Bella Coola River** with the rising tide, known as *A'nxumk·ila*. The canoe travels the distance from the mouth of the river to the mountain **Smaya'na** in a single tide. The canoe named **"Nō'ak·nem"** <note>The "Spirit Canoe" or "God Canoe" which brings the deities from the celestial House of Myths to the human world.</note> stays for four months. Then the canoe named **"Kunkunu'qtstulS"** <note>The "Salmon Canoe" associated with the return of the salmon and the summer season.</note> returns from the country of the Salmon, while the **"Nō'ak·nem"** departs once more. It is said that all the gods of the **House of Myths** <note>original: "Snuqulalsals"; the celestial home where the supreme deity and other gods reside.</note> come to the villages of the **Bella Coola** <note>The Nuxalk people.</note> in the canoe **"Nō'ak·nem."**

The arrival and the departure of these canoes are strictly regulated according to the calendar of the Bella Coola, which for this reason should be explained in connection with their beliefs. The Bella Coola divide the year into two parts, which are separated by the winter and summer solstices. The solstices are periods of indefinite length, between which five months are counted. Each solstice is reckoned, therefore, as approximately six weeks. The names of the months are as follows: —

| Winter Cycle | Summer Cycle |
|---|---|
| **Sēe'mt** (*winter solstice*) | **Sēe'mt** (*summer solstice*) |
| **Sxōle'mx'enem** <note>Month when the salmon return.</note> | **Si'lxum** |
| **Alāō'nstimōt** | **Sexexē'mut** |
| **Siaq'u'm** | **Sinullā'lsemtenem** |
| **Siōiō'lx** | **Tsi sitak'ā'ns tseau Anaūlikuts'ai'x'** <note>Meaning: "When Anaūlikuts'ai'x' opens her door."</note> |
| **Sinō'moak'** | **Lemulē'm** |

The canoe **"Nō'ak·nem"** arrives, and the **"Kunkunu'qtstulS"** leaves, in the month of *Sinullā'lsemtenem*. The canoe **"Nō'ak·nem"** leaves, and the **"Kunkunu'qtstulS"** returns, in the month of *Sxōle'mx'enem*.

At the moment when the canoe **"Nō'ak·nem"** arrives, a deity called **Anaūlikuts'ai'x'** <note>A supernatural female figure associated with the initiation of the winter ceremonial society.</note> (Plate XII, Figs. 1 and 2), who is believed to live in a cave, opens the door of her abode. There is one deity of this name for each village. Her cave is called *Nuskēsiū'tsta*. It is said that one **Anaūlikuts'ai'x'** lives on the mountain **Sqtsl**. Her house is described as a large hollow boulder suspended from the top of the mountain by means of a rope. Each **Anaūlikuts'ai'x'** has an older sister who is called **Nutsē'xenēm**. When the canoe **"Nō'ak·nem"** appears and she opens her door, she steps outside and stands in front of her house, dancing with trembling hands <note>A specific ritual gesture used in the winter dances to indicate spiritual possession or power.</note>. When a person sees her, he faints. His soul is taken into her house and is initiated into the secrets of the **kū'siut** <note>The sacred winter ceremonial society of the Nuxalk, involving masked dances and secret knowledge.</note>.

A particular *Nuskēsiū'tsta* <note>A spirit-cave.</note> is believed to be at the foot of the creek **Anō'nk'**, on the northern side of **North Bentinck Arm**, near the mouth of the Bella Coola River. A woman called **Nustsxoaxlō'stxuil** lives there. She is described as emaciated, with black skin, and wearing a black blanket. A certain chief who lived long ago was the first to see her. He was initiated by her, and after his return, he performed a dance and told what he had seen. Then he died.

<meta>This page continues the description of the supernatural beings of the Nuxalk (Bella Coola) people, moving from the celestial canoes described on the previous page to a specific deity responsible for the initiation of shamans and the transition between the worlds of the living and the dead.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.</header>

There is a special deity who initiates the shamans <note>The term "shaman" refers to a spiritual healer and visionary, known in the Nuxalk language as an *asix·alu'tm*.</note>. His name is **LALAia'il** or **Sxai'ēxoax** <note>original: "LALAia'il or Sxai'ēxoax"; these are names for a powerful spirit of the woods. See Plate XI, Fig. 9</note>. He lives in the woods. He carries a wooden wand wound with red cedar-bark, which he swings in his hands, producing a singing noise. Around his neck he wears a large ring made of strips of bear-skin and red cedar-bark. He sometimes plays in ponds which are believed to be in certain mountains. When he jumps into the water, it boils. When a woman meets him, she begins to menstruate; when a man meets him, his nose begins to bleed. When initiating a person, he touches the chest of the latter with his wand, and paints his face with the design of the rainbow. Then he swings his wand, the noise of which causes the person who hears it to faint. He creates sexual desire in man and animals. A shaman who was initiated by this being told me that he very often sees **LALAia'il**, who tells him who will die and who will fall sick. Sometimes he sees that the body of a person is black. Others he sees dancing on their heads. These are signs that they will die at an early date. I obtained from this man the description of the visit to the country of the ghosts, quoted before (p. 38). He told me that when reaching the country of the dead, he saw the ghosts of his deceased relatives sitting in the house. When they saw him, they began to weep, and said, “Don't come here. We don't want to see you so soon.” While they were speaking to him, the chief's speaker entered the house, and called all the people to come to the dancing-house of the ghosts. One of the ghosts painted his face black and white, and tied long strips of white and red cedar-bark in his hair. The people were called four times. Then they started to go to the dancing-house. The entrance to the door was over a narrow plank. When he had just stepped on the plank, he suddenly saw **LALAia'il**, with his large neck-ring made of red cedar-bark and strips of black bear-skin, who took hold of him, turned him round, and told him to return to his own country, because, if he should once enter the dancing-house, he would not be able to return. Then he revived; and from that time on, **LALAia'il** was his supernatural helper.

**A. Jacobsen** describes this spirit as follows:¹ —

> “The most prominent among the spirits of the shamans is **Kle-klati-ē'il** <note>original: "Kle-klati-ē'il"; another phonetic rendering of the name LALAia'il.</note>. He lives in the woods, where the youth who intends to be initiated tries to find him. When the spirit meets him, the youth faints. When he recovers, he begins to sing a song, the tune and words of which have been given to him by the spirit. Now he has become a shaman, who uses this song in all his incantations; but he does not retain it throughout life, because he meets his guardian spirit almost every year, and then he receives new songs. The Indians believe that **Kle-klati-ē'il** has human shape, but he is clothed in cedar-bark, and wears a great many rings of cedar-bark.”

---

<note>¹ Proceedings of the Berlin Society for Anthropology, Ethnology, and Prehistory, 1894, p. 104. <note>original: "Verhandlungen der Berliner Gesellschaft für Anthropologie, Ethnologie und Urgeschichte"</note></note>

<summary>This page details the deity LALAia'il, a powerful forest spirit who initiates shamans through visions, ritual wands, and rainbow face-painting. It also recounts a shaman's vision of the afterlife, where LALAia'il intervenes to prevent the living from entering the permanent realm of the ghosts.</summary>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the supernatural beings who initiate shamans among the Nuxalk people, further identifying the spirit LALAia'il (also known as Sxai'ēxoax) and introducing a mythological narrative regarding the Sun's role in granting spiritual power.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>

<header>BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.</header>

Some of these he gives to the shaman <note>A spiritual healer and visionary, known in the Nuxalk language as an *asix·alu'tm*.</note>. . . . A third spirit is the **Skaia** <note>original: "Sxaî'êxoax"; a powerful spirit of the woods or water, often associated with the salmon.</note>, which is believed to live in rivers, and to have the shape of a salmon. When a shaman who is inspired by this spirit makes an incantation in a house, singing and dancing, as all the shamans do, whoever approaches the house and hears the song turns back. They believe that whoever passes the house during the incantation will be punished with death by the spirit **Skaia**."

I believe this spirit is identical with **Lalaia'il**, who, as stated before, has the second name **Sxaî'êxoax**. According to **Jacobsen** <note>Adrian Jacobsen (1853–1947) was a Norwegian explorer and collector who gathered ethnographic materials from the Northwest Coast.</note>, some shamans are initiated by the ghosts. He says that the ghosts are believed to have bald heads and blue faces. I am under the impression, however, that the people initiated by them are not shamans, but **kŭ'siut** dancers <note>The *kŭ'siut* are members of a prestigious Nuxalk secret society who perform sacred dances during the winter ceremonial season.</note> (see Chap. VI).

Another being who initiates shamans is described in the following tradition: —

Once upon a time there was a man and a woman who had four sons. The three elder ones died. Then the father and mother and the youngest brother were very unhappy, and the old people cried for grief until they died. The young man was now left all alone. He left his village, intending to go away and never to return. He pulled his blanket over his head and walked on. Sometimes he would stop to pray. He lived on the meat of mountain-goats which he shot. He built a small hut high up on the mountains, and dried the meat. He was crying and praying all the time. He prayed to the **Sun** to give him a gift which would restore his happiness. One day early in the morning he ascended the mountain. He addressed the rising **Sun**, saying, "Look at me, how unhappy I am." After he had gone a short distance he came to a ravine. The bed of the ravine was filled with pretty pebbles. There he met a beautiful man, who was no other than the **Sun**, who had descended from the sky. He had caused the water of the creek that runs through the ravine to disappear. When the young man saw the stranger from a distance, he thought, "He seems to be looking for me." He went nearer; and when they met, the **Sun** said, "I am the one to whom you are praying all the time, and I came to help you. Now be happy. When you open your mouth and speak to me, I know your thoughts at once. I help those who address themselves to me. Take this." With these words he handed the young man a switch <note>A thin, flexible wooden wand or rod used for spiritual or ritual purposes.</note> carved in the shape of a man. The **Sun** was carrying it under his arm, the point of the switch directed downward. "Fold your arms and hold this switch to your chest, and then return to the village. When you approach any one, hide the switch under your arm. You will find a person who wears a nose-ornament of beautiful green color <note>Likely made of abalone shell or copper, which were highly prized materials used in status-bearing jewelry.</note>. Then you must try to hit the ornament with this switch, and throw it to your right side."

<summary>This page details the characteristics of the salmon-shaped spirit Skaia and provides a mythological account of a grieving man who receives a carved ritual wand from the Sun to restore his happiness and provide him with spiritual power.</summary>

<keywords>Skaia, Sxaî'êxoax, shamanism, Sun deity, ritual objects, Nuxalk mythology, mourning rituals, Jacobsen, kŭ'siut society</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of a young man seeking spiritual power after the death of his family. It details his encounter with a spirit of the dead and introduces two other powerful sources of shamanic power: the spirit L'ētsa'aplēLana and the supernatural creature known as the Sisiutl.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>

<header>BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE NUXALK INDIANS.</header>

He walked on, and after a while he noticed a man sitting at a distance. Then he hid the switch under his arm. When he came near, he saw that the man wore a large green nose-ring. He hit it with his switch, and threw it to the right-hand side. Then the man said, "You have attained me as your supernatural helper. Your name shall be <term>S'a'tema</term> <note>original: "S'a'tema"; a name derived from the Nuxalk word *a'tema*, meaning "dead."</note>. Many people have seen me, but nobody has done what you did. If you had not struck my nose-ornament, you would have died on seeing me. You shall have the power to heal the sick by the touch of your hands. Whenever a person dies and is put into a box, after the box has been placed in the burial-ground, go there. You will find me sitting on the coffin. If then you knock the nose-ring from out of my nose, I shall leave, and the dead will revive. He will break the box, and will arise."

Then the young man felt very glad. He returned to the village, and by following the instructions of the spirit he resuscitated the dead. He was given many blankets, and the men whom he had resuscitated gave him their daughters in marriage.

Other <term>shamans</term> <note>Traditional spiritual healers and visionaries, known in Nuxalk as *asix·alu'tm*.</note> are initiated by <term>L'ētsa'aplēLana</term> <note>A powerful female spirit associated with song and healing.</note>. The same man who gave me the record of his supposed visit to the country of the ghosts (see p. 38) told me that at another time he saw <term>L'ētsa'aplēLana</term> flying in the air outside of his house. She wore a ring of red cedar-bark around her neck. She was turning round all the time. Songs were coming from all parts of her body. Although she did not open her mouth, it sounded as though a great many people were singing. She gave him a song, or, as the narrator expressed it, "she threw a song into his body." At that time he was sick, suffering from a wound in his leg inflicted by an axe. He said four days after meeting the spirit he was able to walk, and since that time she has assisted him in curing diseases.

The <term>Sisiutl</term> <note>original: "sī'siuL"; a powerful, double-headed sea serpent found in the mythology of many Indigenous peoples of the Pacific Northwest Coast, often possessing transformative powers.</note> is another helper of the <term>shaman</term>, and the means of curing disease (see p. 28). It appears that it obtains its supernatural power from the fact that it lives in the water in which the supreme deity washes her face. When a person sees a <term>Sisiutl</term>, he should throw sand on it, by which means he will be able to catch it. Its skin is so hard that it cannot be pierced with a spear or knife. The person who catches it should not try to cut it with his knife, but should stretch his hand backward, and thus he will find the leaf of a holly <note>Likely referring to the Oregon Grape or a similar prickly-leaved plant used in ritual cleansing and protection.</note>, which is the only thing that can cut its skin. He should not touch the <term>Sisiutl</term> with his hands, but hold it with hemlock twigs. He should wrap it in white cedar-bark <note>The inner bark of the Western Red Cedar, which is highly significant in sacred and ceremonial contexts.</note> and tie it up in his blanket. If it is not thus tied up, it will disappear. It must not be taken into the house, but should be placed in a small box and hidden under stones, or buried in a hole under the root of a tree. It is a most potent means of curing disease. Sick people will buy small pieces of the <term>Sisiutl</term>, for which

<summary>This page describes various supernatural sources of healing power, including a spirit of the dead who allows a shaman to resuscitate the deceased, the musical spirit L'ētsa'aplēLana, and the legendary double-headed serpent, the Sisiutl.</summary>

they pay high prices <note>Referring to the scales or parts of the supernatural serpent described on the previous page.</note>. The piece is thrown into water, in which it is kept for four days. Then the water is used for washing the body. If a healthy person uses this water, he will live to an old age. Sick persons chew the white cedar-bark in which it is wrapped up, in order to regain health. They must not swallow the cedar-bark, but only the saliva that gathers in their mouths. A person who has chewed the cedar-bark becomes invulnerable. The eye of the <term>Sisiutl</term> <note>original: "si'siuL"; a powerful, double-headed sea serpent capable of transforming into a canoe or fish, often associated with shamanic power and protection.</note> is described as about a foot in diameter, and as transparent as rock crystal.

<term>Toa'lal'it</term> <note>A benevolent spirit and the master of the mountain goats who grants success and protection to hunters.</note> is the spirit who protects the mountain-goat hunter. He himself is invisible; but great hunters sometimes see his hat, his moccasins, or his mountain staff moving about. The following tradition describes some of his characteristics: —

The Raven and the Lynx lived in one house, each occupying one side. Early in the morning the Raven went out to catch salmon with the harpoon. He was very successful, and carried the fish home. Toa'lal'it watched the Raven, who, when he arrived at the house, roasted his salmon. The children of the Lynx were sitting near by, and looking at the Raven while he was roasting the fish. They wished to participate in the meal, but he did not give them anything. Then the children were very sad. Now the Lynx made up his mind to make arrows and to go hunting mountain-goat. He went out and cut some wood for his arrows; then he told his wife to make a quiver, which he called <term>Ts’ō’lapEla</term> <note>The Nuxalk name for a traditional quiver woven from cedar-bark.</note>. She did so, and wove a quiver of cedar-bark. The Lynx was quite impatient for the sun to rise, so eager was he to start hunting. Early in the morning he arose and ascended the mountain. When he reached the limit of the trees, he sat down on a flat rock and looked for goats. Then he saw the staff of a man who was coming down the mountain. He did not see the person himself. He thought, "Who is that? Who may be hunting here?" The stick approached him; and when it came near, he saw Toa'lal'it, who wore a large hat. His hat was named <term>Q!pōL</term> <note>meaning a "barren mountain-top."</note>. Toa'lal'it reached the Lynx, and sat down opposite him. Neither spoke a word. After a while Toa'lal'it arose, took the arrows of the Lynx, and said, "How beautiful these arrows are!" He took up one after another until he had looked at all the four arrows of the Lynx. Then he asked, "Who made these arrows?" The Lynx did not reply. Toa'lal'it asked again, "Who made these arrows?" Then Lynx replied, "Toa'lal'it made my arrows." Then Toa'lal'it was very much pleased, and said, "Is he the one who made your arrows?" — "Certainly," replied Lynx. Then Toa'lal'it took his arrows and threw one after another down the mountain, and said to the Lynx, "Now go down the mountain and look at your arrows. If you spoke the truth, every

<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 45, where the spirit Toa'lal'it observes the interactions between the Raven and the Lynx. It describes the Lynx’s successful hunt under the spirit's guidance and the Raven’s subsequent failure to replicate that success.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>

# BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE NUXALK INDIANS. <note>original: "BELLA COOLA INDIANS"; Nuxalk is the contemporary and preferred name for the indigenous people of the Bella Coola Valley in British Columbia.</note>

"...one of them will have killed a large mountain-goat." The Lynx descended the mountain, and saw that every one of his arrows had killed a goat. Then he was glad, because now he had food for his children. He jumped and danced for joy. And the <term>Master of Mountain Goats</term> <note>original: "Toa'laL'it"; a powerful supernatural protector who governs the success of mountain-goat hunters.</note> said, "I am the Master of Mountain Goats. I am so called because I am the hunter of mountain-goats. Now return to your village. From now on, I am your supernatural protector. The next time you go hunting, and you do not find any mountain-goats, sit down and throw your arrows down the mountain. Every one will kill a goat. But do not lose those arrows. If you should lose them, you would never kill another mountain-goat."

The mountain-goats were so large, that Lynx took only their fat, which he put into his quiver and climbed down the mountain. He arrived at his house in the afternoon. He left his quiver outside, and the Raven saw him coming in. The Lynx sat down by the side of his wife. He did not say a word. His wife and her children also received him in silence. <note>In many Indigenous oral traditions, successful hunters are expected to demonstrate humility and restraint upon returning from a hunt.</note> In the evening, when it was dark, he said to his wife, "Go and fetch my quiver. It is hanging outside on a stick." She went there and tried to lift it, but it was too heavy. She returned to her husband and told him that she was not strong enough to lift it, and asked him to fetch it himself. He said, "It is not heavy." He went out himself and brought it in. He opened it and took out the fat; and he gave some of it to his children, but he did not give any to the Raven's children. Then the Raven was very sad. After a little while, the Raven's children began to cry, because they wanted some of the fat.

Early the next morning the Lynx went to fetch the meat of the mountain-goat. The Raven watched him, and saw where he went. When he saw that the Lynx had killed mountain-goats, he made up his mind to go hunting too. He told his wife to make him a quiver while he went out to cut wood for his arrows. In the evening the quiver and the arrows were done. In the afternoon the Lynx came home, bringing the meat of the mountain-goats. 

Early the next morning the Raven started, following the tracks of the Lynx. He reached the place where the Lynx had sat down. He sat there, and placed his arrows by his side. He looked around for mountain-goats. After some time he saw a staff moving along in the distance. It approached, and soon he saw a man coming down the mountain. It was the <term>Master of Mountain Goats</term>. He reached him, and sat down opposite the Raven. They did not speak a word. After a while the Master of Mountain Goats arose, took up the arrows, and said, "Man, your arrows are beautiful. Who made them?" The Raven did not reply. Then the Master of Mountain Goats said again, "Tell me who made your arrows." Then the Raven answered, "The name of the man who makes my arrows is Raven." <note>By naming himself in a boastful or direct manner, Raven fails to show the humility required by the spirit protector.</note> Then the Master of Mountain Goats took the arrows, threw them down the mountain, and said to the Raven, "You are bad!" And he turned back, and ascended the mountain. The Raven went down the mountain, trying to find his arrows.

<summary>This page concludes the story of Lynx's successful mountain-goat hunt under the protection of the spirit Toa'lal'it and contrasts it with the Raven's failed attempt. The Raven's lack of humility and his attempts to mimic the Lynx lead to his rejection by the spirit.</summary>
