the cry, "Hoip!" <note>A ritual exclamation used by the assistants to calm and control the possessed initiate.</note> which is intended to pacify the **Cannibal**. They wear masks <note>The masks mentioned here—Plate XII, Figs. 4 and 5—typically represent the supernatural assistants of the spirit world who help manage the wild energy of the initiate.</note>. The people try to throw a noose over his head; but he throws it off, refusing to be captured. Then the people shout, "Ya'i!" beating time rapidly. The **Cannibal** tears the ropes that hold him, and disappears. Then the people follow him, and search for him until they find him. As soon as they approach, he attacks them, but gradually he becomes quieter. Then the <term>Speaker</term> <note>Nuxalk: Alk". An official herald who directs the movements of the ceremony and speaks for the host or the spirits.</note> invites the people to the dancing-house, saying, **"Dance, dancers!"** <note>original Nuxalk: "Ip'a'nap kukusiau't!"</note> Now the **Cannibal** opens the door, accompanied by his assistants. He wears the mask <note>Plate XII, Fig. 3 refers to the primary ceremonial mask of the Cannibal dancer, often depicting a terrifying humanoid face with bloody accents.</note> shown in Plate XII, Fig. 3. At once a number of women arise, ready to surround him as soon as he enters the house. They are called <term>Alal'au'ltemx</term> <note>A specialized group of female ritual attendants who dance to contain the Cannibal's power.</note>. The speaker, who holds a staff, arises and shouts, **"Act like a real Cannibal!"** <note>original Nuxalk: "Ō n yä'liwatimötx AlqöLayak'ai's!" This command urges the initiate to demonstrate the full power of the spirit that has seized him.</note> The **Cannibal** utters his cry, "Hoaaä, hoaaä, hoaaä!" ending with a long deep growl. He stays in the doorway for a long time. Then he steps into the house, turns his left shoulder outward once, bites one man on the right-hand side of the house, and one on the left-hand side, and then performs his circuit of the fire, leaving the fire to his left <note>Movement in a counter-clockwise direction is common in these ceremonies to represent the movements of the spirits.</note>. In dancing he holds his fists in front of his chest, one on each side. He moves in a stooping position, raising his feet very high. He dances for four nights, during which time the people try to pacify him by means of songs and dances. After he has first been induced to enter the house, he becomes quieter. Then he tells the people what he has seen during his absence, and the song-leader makes a song on the subject. The women do not learn about the events that took place during his absence until they hear the song. After four days the ceremony of exorcising the <term>S'a'Lpsta</term> <note>A supernatural monster or spirit-force that possesses the dancer. This is illustrated in Plate XII, Figs. 6 and 7.</note>, the monster that possesses him, is performed. A large dish is ornamented with red cedar-bark; and the assistant of the <term>S'a'Lpsta</term> <note>A ritual specialist, shown in Plate XII, Fig. 8, who manages the physical manifestation of the spirit.</note>, by means of incantations, makes the **Cannibal** vomit the snake, or the wolf, or the eagle, that possesses him. Then he is seen to carry the head of the animal under his left arm, while the body is seen in the rear, being held by his assistants <note>This theatrical display uses a carved puppet or effigy to represent the spirit leaving the human body.</note>. Then this animal is seen to vomit flesh and blood into the dish which has been prepared for the purpose. The **Cannibal's** assistants cover the body of the animal with down, while they cry from time to time, "Hoip!" Then the people beat time very rapidly, and suddenly the <term>S'a'Lpsta</term> has disappeared. The people beat time four times, and after this the **Cannibal** has become like an ordinary man. He walks around the fire, which he keeps to his right, and says, **"Now the state of possession has left me."** <note>original Nuxalk: "Ba'xölla." This term refers to the return to a secular, human state after being in the sacred, "wild" state of the spirit world.</note> The contents of the dish are thrown into the water, but the dish itself is burned in the dancing-house. It is supposed that it is thus conveyed to heaven. After the dish has been burned, the **Cannibal** does not dance any more. Then a bed is prepared for him in the rear of the house, in which he must stay for four days. After this, he is

allowed to re-enter the houses; but before doing so for the first time, he must stop at the door and shout, "Wa, wa, wa!" Then he jumps over the threshold. Accompanied by many men, he is led, after four days more, to the river for final purification. He is pushed into the water, but struggles to free himself. He tries to duck his companions, and utters his cries. Finally he is led back. Then he weeps, because the spirit has left him entirely. The people beat time while he is going back to the house. Then he is offered a piece of salmon, which is placed on his throat, while the people shout, "Hoip, hoip!" <note>A ritual exclamation used to signal transitions in the dancer’s state of being.</note> The salmon is taken away again, and after some time he is allowed to take one bite. Then he must lie down. After the dancing season is ended, he is again allowed to eat in the same way as other people do. The piece of salmon that was placed on his throat is eaten by children as a protection against sickness. Finally a ceremony is performed which is called "taking the lip of the <term>S'ā'Lpsta</term> <note>A supernatural entity or spirit-force associated with the Cannibal dancer; the 'lip' represents the tangible essence of the spirit that possessed the initiate.</note> out of the **Cannibal's** body." While this is being done, the people cry, "Hoip!" Then they throw the lip upon another **Cannibal**, who at once falls into an ecstasy. Then the shaman takes it back and throws it up towards the sky, where it disappears. The dish and the spoon that the **Cannibal** used during his ecstasy are destroyed. For four years he must wear a small ring of red cedar-bark <note>Red cedar-bark is a sacred material used in Northwest Coast ceremonies to signify a person’s contact with the supernatural world.</note>, in summer as well as in winter.

The <term>Ōlx</term> <note>A high-ranking ritual dancer and member of a secret society, often associated with birds and the upper world.</note> is also initiated in heaven by the being described before (p. 35). There are different traditions regarding his initiation among different clans. Some **Ōlx**, during their performances, walk ropes which are stretched through the house. When he returns from his initiation, small whistles, called <term>ēaLī'laxa</term> <note>Nuxalk: literal meaning "eagle-voices." These hidden whistles are used by assistants to represent the cries of spirits.</note>, which imitate the voices of eagles, are heard outside of the houses. Then the **Ōlx** enters in a state of ecstasy, scratching the people who assemble to hold him, and tearing their blankets. Finally the people succeed in placing a blanket over his head, and he begins to come to his senses. When he enters the house, the people beat time, then he teaches the chorus-leader a song. The mask worn by the **Ōlx** is quite large, and characterized by a large nose <note>See Plate XII, Fig. 9; the exaggerated nose is a distinctive feature of this specific spirit mask.</note>. He carries a dancing-club <note>See Plate XII, Fig. 10; these clubs were often carved with ancestral or spirit motifs.</note>, and rings made of red cedar-bark. He is accompanied by two assistants, who also wear masks.

## VII.

We will now discuss the probable origin of the mythology described in the preceding pages. In order to do so, it is necessary to make a brief statement in regard to the social organization of the neighboring tribes. In another paper¹ I have fully discussed the considerable amount of borrowing that has taken place among the Coast tribes, and the relation of their

---

<note>original: "Indianische Sagen von der nord-pacifischen Küste Amerikas" — Indian Legends of the North Pacific Coast of America, Berlin, 1895, pp. vi + 363.</note>

<meta>This page continues Boas’s analysis of cultural transmission, shifting from the specific details of Nuxalk (Bella Coola) mythology and ritual to a broader comparative study of their social structures against those of their neighbors.</meta>

## BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS. 121
### 16—MEMOIRS OF THE AMERICAN MUSEUM OF NATURAL HISTORY, VOL. II. NOV., 1898.

mythologies to those of the interior. It is unnecessary to revert to this subject here. The similarity of the Bella Coola <note>The people today known as the Nuxalk.</note> legends to those of the other Coast tribes on the one hand, and to the traditions of the Athapascan <note>A broad group of interior Indigenous peoples, including the Dené.</note> tribes on the other, is evident.

It is, however, important to compare their social organization with that of the neighboring tribes, in order to gain a clearer understanding of the origin of their peculiar organization. As stated before, the Bella Coola are divided into village communities, which are organized on an endogamic <note>A practice where individuals marry within their own local community or social group rather than marrying outside of it.</note> basis. Each village community has its tradition, which is represented in certain ceremonies. The supernatural beings which play a part in these traditions are personified by certain dancers. Other Coast tribes have a much more complex organization. The Tlingit <note>Lingít</note> and Haida <note>X̱aayda</note> are divided into two clans, each of which is subdivided into a great many families, which, as it would seem, were originally village communities. This opinion is based on the fact that the names of many of the families must be translated as "inhabitants of such and such a place." The two clans are present in all the villages of the tribe, each family belonging to either one clan or the other. The Tsimshian <note>Ts'msyen</note> have the same system, except that the number of clans is four instead of two. Each tribe is divided into families, which embrace the inhabitants of a certain region; but all the families of the whole tribe are classified according to the four clans. Among the Haida, Tlingit, and Tsimshian, descent is purely maternal <note>Inheritance, names, and social standing are passed down through the mother’s line.</note>; <unclear>a</unclear>mong the northern Kwakiutl <note>Kwakwaka'wakw</note> tribes, conditions are somewhat different, according to observations made by Dr. Livingston Farrand. Here we have village communities which are subdivided according to four clans in the same way as those of the Tsimshian, but descent is not purely maternal. There is a strong preponderance of the latter form of descent, but parents are at liberty to place their children in either the paternal or maternal clan. The preponderance of maternal descent is, however, so strong, that from my previous occasional inquiries I drew the inference that descent was purely maternal.

Among the southern Kwakiutl tribes the families constituting a village community are subdivided into a number of clans, but each clan is confined to one village. We do not find a limited number of clans pervading the whole tribe, as we do among the northern tribes. An analysis of the social organization of this tribe has shown that the present organization has evidently developed from a previous simpler state, in which the tribe was divided into single village communities. The present more complex organization resulted from the amalgamation of various villages. Owing to the influence of the totemism <note>A social system where families or clans identify with a specific animal or spirit (totem) that serves as their emblem and guardian.</note> of the northern tribes, each village community adopted a crest, which in course of time became the totem of the clan.<gloss>1</gloss> The Kwakiutl have a peculiar organization, which may be considered a transitional stage between

->BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.<-

maternal and paternal institutions. Descent is in the paternal line; but a man, at the time of his marriage, receives his father-in-law's crest <note>A "crest" is a heraldic symbol, usually representing an animal or supernatural being, which signifies a family's ancestry, status, and inherited rights.</note> as a dowry, which he holds in trust for his son, so that actually each individual inherits the crest of his maternal grandfather. The clans are exogamic <note>Exogamic refers to the practice of marrying outside of one's own social or family group.</note>.

The organization of the Salish tribes of the southern coast, who are linguistically closely related to the Bella Coola <note>Today known as the Nuxalk.</note>, is somewhat similar to that of the Kwakiutl <note>Today known as the Kwakwaka’wakw.</note>. They are divided into village communities, a few of which have amalgamated <note>Merged or united.</note>, as, for instance, among the Salish tribes of Vancouver Island, among whom the tribe consists of a number of septs <note>A "sept" is a branch of a family or a subgroup within a larger tribe.</note>, each of which owns a separate village. Here the influence of northern totemism <note>The system of belief where groups have a spiritual connection to a "totem," such as an animal or plant.</note> is very much weaker. While most of the village communities have certain crests, these do not play so important a part in the social life of the tribe or in its mythology as they do among the Kwakiutl, and the village communities are not exogamic. The Salish tribes of the interior are organized in very loose village communities without any trace of totem.

The fundamental difference between the northern tribes and the southern tribes consists, therefore, in the fact that the northern tribes have a limited number of clans which are present in all the villages, while among the southern tribes the village community is the only unit of organization.

The organization of the Bella Coola resembles most closely that of the Coast Salish tribes of southern British Columbia. In both cases the tribe is divided into village communities, which possess crests and traditions. This latter feature is, however, very much more strongly developed among the Bella Coola than among the southern tribes. They differ in their laws of intermarriage. While among the southern Coast Salish tribes there is a tendency to exogamy, the Bella Coola have developed a system of endogamy <note>Endogamy is the practice of marrying only within the limits of a local community or social group.</note>.

The tribes of the Coast Salish of the Gulf of Georgia claim descent from mythical ancestors, who are believed to have originated at the place which the tribe now inhabit. A number of traditions of this kind bear evidence of having been derived from historical events. Some of the tribes in the delta of Fraser River have traditions which refer to the amalgamation of tribes who descended from the mountains, and who are described as the descendants of animals living in the mountains, and of the natives of the delta.¹ I believe that the tribal traditions of the Bella Coola which were told in a previous chapter also bear evidence of the historical fates of the tribe. It is very remarkable that the important tradition of Tōtōsō'nx gives Fraser River as the place to which he descended from heaven. In another tradition, Bute Inlet is given as the place at which one of the Bella Coola tribes originated. In still another one, Skeena River is mentioned as the home of one of the tribes (see p. 69). I do not doubt that these allusions to territory

### BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS. 123

The territories inhabited by <term>Salish</term><note>A large group of Indigenous nations in the Pacific Northwest who share related languages.</note> tribes contain stories that refer to the early separation of the <term>Bella Coola</term><note>The Nuxalk people.</note> tribe from their relatives near the Gulf of Georgia. These traditions have preserved the memory of a time when part of the tribe emigrated from that southern territory. It also seems likely that the mention of one family’s origin on the Skeena River refers to historical mixing with the tribes living in northern British Columbia.

The traditions of these tribes also describe the specific styles of houses used by the ancestors of certain village communities. It is interesting to note that some of these houses match the <term>subterranean lodges</term><note>Often called "pit houses," these were dwellings built partially underground for insulation.</note> used by the <term>Chilcotin</term><note>The Tsilhqot’in people.</note>, while others match the tents used by the <term>Carriers</term><note>The Dakelh people.</note>. It is said that the ancestor of the <term>Nullē'ix</term> used the subterranean lodge called the <term>tsi'pa</term> (see p. 87), while the ancestor of the <term>Nusq!E'lst</term> used the skin lodge called the <term>sk·ma</term> (see p. 64).

Since the Bella Coola retain the basic social traits of their southern relatives, and since their traditions point to a migration from that region—and further, since linguistic evidence proves the Bella Coola and the Coast Salish once lived in adjacent areas—we are justified in assuming that the general culture of the Bella Coola at the time of their migration must have resembled that of the Coast Salish. This raises a question: How did the unique <term>endogamic system</term><note>A social system where marriage is required to happen within a specific group or community.</note> and the complex mythology of the Bella Coola develop from the much simpler forms found among the Coast Salish?

One of the most remarkable features of the inner life of the tribes on the northern coast of British Columbia is the great importance of the **clan legend**. These stories are considered some of the most valuable properties of a clan or family. They are guarded as carefully as physical wealth. If someone who is not a member of the clan tries to tell the tradition as their own, it is considered a very grave offense against property rights. To an Indigenous person, possessing a clan tradition is one of their most important ancestral rights. Therefore, when the Bella Coola settled on the Bella Coola River and encountered the northern Coast tribes, the lack of a highly developed clan tradition must have felt like a serious disadvantage. The physical appearance of the Bella Coola proves that they must have intermarried extensively with the <term>Bella Bella</term><note>The Heiltsuk people.</note> at one time. Through these marriages, the unique customs of the Coast tribes were first introduced to them. This is proven by the fact that many mythological names have a <term>Kwakiutl</term><note>The Kwakwaka’wakw people.</note> origin. For example, the name for their supreme deity, **Smai'yakila**, is a Kwakiutl word meaning "the one who must be worshipped." The name **Ō'mq'ōmkilik·a** is also of Kwakiutl origin and can be translated as "the wealthiest one." The great frequency of—

Along with these names and customs, the clan traditions must have found their way to the Bella Coola, but their social organization differed fundamentally from that of the <term>Bella Bella</term><note>The Heiltsuk people.</note> branch of the Kwakiutl. While the latter—owing to intimate contact with the northern tribes—had adopted the four-clan system with prevalent **maternal descent** <note>Inheritance of status, names, and rights through the mother's family line.</note>, the Bella Coola were still distinctly divided into village communities that were not **exogamic** <note>Refers to "exogamy," the custom of being required to marry outside of one's own social or family group.</note>. It seems very likely that the jealousy with which the ownership of a clan tradition was guarded by the Coast tribes was introduced to the Bella Coola at a very early stage. 

Two means were available for preventing outsiders from acquiring these family traditions. Among the Coast tribes with prevailing maternal institutions, where only a limited number of clans existed, the ordinary law of inheritance was sufficient to keep the tradition within the clan. This was not the case among the Bella Coola. If their organization in early times was similar to that of the <term>Coast Salish</term><note>The broader linguistic and cultural group to which the Nuxalk belong.</note>, it is likely that a child was considered a member of both the father's and the mother's family, even though the young woman generally moved to the village occupied by her husband. If the child belonged to the families of both parents, they had the right to use the traditions of either family; consequently, in the course of a few generations, the traditions acquired by each family would have spread practically over the whole tribe. There were only two possible methods to avoid this dilution. The first was to prevent marriages outside of the village community; this method would seem the most natural for a tribe organized into village communities where members were allowed to intermarry. The other method would have been to regulate the laws of inheritance in such a way that the child had to

<meta>This page continues Boas's analysis of the Nuxalk (Bella Coola) social structure, focusing on the shift from Salish village systems to a unique marriage and inheritance model influenced by neighboring coastal tribes.</meta>

### BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS. 125

Follow either father or mother, but that it had not the right to use the property of both parents. It seems to my mind that the former method was more likely to develop under the existing social conditions, and that to this reason we must ascribe the development of an <term>endogamic</term><note>A system where marriage is required to occur within a specific social group, such as a village or clan.</note> system among the <term>Bella Coola</term><note>The Nuxalk people.</note>. The occurrence of endogamic marriage among this tribe is quite isolated on the Pacific coast. All the other tribes have <term>exogamic</term><note>A system where marriage must occur outside of one's own social group or clan to preserve alliances and distribute property.</note> institutions, and by this means preserve their property rights. It is interesting to note that the southern <term>Kwakiutl</term><note>The Kwakwaka'wakw people.</note>, who originally seem to have been organized in village communities, have adopted exogamic institutions; but there is a notable difference, in the organization of the village community, between the Bella Coola and the Kwakiutl. Among the Bella Coola we generally find four ancestors to each village—usually three men and one woman. It is true that these are generally called brothers and sister, but they were created independently by <term>SENX</term><note>Also known as Alquntam, the supreme deity and the Sun in Nuxalk theology.</note>, and are therefore not necessarily considered as blood relations. Among the Kwakiutl the village community are considered the descendants of one single being; consequently, among the latter tribe they are all relatives, who are forbidden to intermarry; while among the Bella Coola they are not relatives, and may intermarry.

My inference is, therefore, that the curious social system of the Bella Coola developed through the influence of the customs of the Coast tribes upon the loose social unit of the <term>Salish</term><note>The broader linguistic and cultural group to which the Nuxalk belong; most Salish tribes live further south.</note> village community. The possession of clan traditions was felt as a great advantage, and consequently the desire developed to possess clan traditions. These were acquired partly by intermarriage with the Coast tribes, as is shown by the fact that many of these traditions are borrowed from these tribes, partly by independent invention. The desire to guard the traditions which were once acquired led to the development of endogamic institutions, in order to prevent the spread of the traditions over the whole tribe.

The jealousy with which the traditions are guarded has had the effect of making each family try to prevent other families from knowing its own clan tradition. For this reason the clan traditions of the whole tribe are remarkably inconsistent. We find, for instance, that the well-known raven legend of the northern Coast tribes has been utilized by many families as a family tradition. But while one family uses one part of the tradition, other families use other parts of the same tradition. Thus it happens that among the Bella Coola we find the most contradictory myths in regard to important events in the world’s history. Some families maintain that the <term>Raven</term><note>A central figure and transformer in Northwest Coast mythology, often credited with bringing light to the world.</note> liberated the sun, while, according to another one, the <term>Mink</term><note>Another prominent transformer and trickster figure in indigenous Northwest Coast oral histories.</note> was essential in bringing about the present state of affairs. Still others say that <term>Tōtōsō’nx</term><note>A powerful mythological figure and transformer in Nuxalk tradition.</note>, during his travels, caused the sun to appear. The discrepancies in the traditions referring to the visit of the Mink and <term>Wasp</term> to their father, the Sun, are also very remarkable. Although a considerable amount of

<summary>Boas argues that the Nuxalk developed a unique system of marrying within the village (endogamy) to protect exclusive family myths and property. This secrecy resulted in highly contradictory tribal mythologies, with different families claiming different heroes—such as the Raven, Mink, or Tōtōsō’nx—were responsible for the creation of the sun.</summary>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues Boas’s analysis of the Nuxalk (Bella Coola) worldview, moving from social organization to the specific nature of their mythology, its systematic structure, and its relationship to the rituals of neighboring tribes.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>

->BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS.<-

contradiction is inherent in all the mythologies of the North Pacific coast, they nowhere reach such a degree as among the <term>Bella Coola</term><note>The Nuxalk people of the central British Columbia coast.</note> ; and I presume the fact that the traditions are kept secret by the various families accounts for this curious condition.

The prayers of the Bella Coola directed to <term>SENX</term><note>The Sun deity and supreme creator, also known as Alquntam.</note> or <term>TA'ATA</term><note>Literally "Our Father," a title for the supreme deity.</note> bear a remarkable resemblance to the prayers of the <term>Tsimshian</term><note>An Indigenous group neighboring the Nuxalk to the north.</note> addressed to <term>Laxha</term><note>The Tsimshian word for the sky or the personified heaven.</note>, the sky. In both tribes we find the idea that when the Sun wipes his face it will be clear weather, and man will be happy ; consequently the prayer to the deity "to wipe his face" occurs quite often.

One of the most important customs that the Bella Coola borrowed from the Coast tribes is the *kū'siut* ceremonial, with which are connected the various secret societies, particularly the custom of ceremonial cannibalism. The ceremonies and the paraphernalia used by the Bella Coola and by the <term>Kwakiutl</term><note>The Kwakwaka'wakw people.</note> are practically identical. I told above (p. 34) the legend explaining the origin of cannibalism. Among the Bella Bella and Kwakiutl, another tradition is told to explain the origin of this custom. The tradition tells of a spirit called <term>Baxbakualanuxsi'waē</term><note>A powerful, man-eating spirit in Kwakwaka'wakw mythology who lives in the mountains and initiates members of the Hamat'sa (Cannibal) society.</note>, who lives in the forest, and who initiates the members of the Cannibal Society. The series of traditions clustering around this being differ fundamentally from those referring to the Cannibal Society of the Bella Coola. The custom has also spread to the Tsimshian, who say that the secret societies were introduced by a hunter who was taken into the inside of a cliff by a white bear. Inside he saw a house, in which the various societies were performing their ceremonies. It appears, therefore, that the same ritual which is practised by three distinct tribes is explained by three fundamentally distinct myths ; and we must conclude that in this case the ritual is older than the myth,—that the latter has been invented in order to explain customs that were borrowed from foreign tribes, so that the ritual is the primary phenomenon, while the myth is secondary.^1

These considerations explain some of the psychological motives for the development of certain traditions and myths, as well as the curious inconsistency of the clan traditions of the Bella Coola. They do not, however, explain the most fundamental characteristic of the traditions of the tribe. I pointed out in the third chapter of this paper, that, notwithstanding the numerous contradictions contained in family legends, the conception of the word and the functions of the various deities are so well defined that we must consider the mythology of this tribe vastly superior to that of the neighboring tribes. While the latter believe in a great many spirits which are not co-ordinated, we have here a system of deities. The existence of a systematic mythology among the Bella Coola proves that under favorable conditions the advance from the lower forms of beliefs to higher forms may be a very rapid one.

^1 See Report of the U. S. National Museum for 1895, pp. 660 ff.

<summary>Boas argues that Nuxalk ritual practices, such as the kū'siut ceremonial, were borrowed from neighbors and later given unique mythological explanations, resulting in a highly systematic yet contradictory theology.</summary>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page concludes Boas’s analysis of the Nuxalk (Bella Coola) mythological system. He moves from describing specific rituals and deities to a broader discussion on the methodology of anthropology, arguing that a culture must be studied as a whole—combining both historical origins and universal human psychology—to be truly understood.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

->BOAS, THE MYTHOLOGY OF THE BELLA COOLA INDIANS. 127<-

Our analysis shows that this system cannot be considered as something simply brought in from the outside, but that it probably developed among the <term>Bella Coola</term><note>The Nuxalk people of the central British Columbia coast. At the time of Boas's writing, they were commonly referred to by the Heiltsuk name "Bella Coola."</note> themselves. After they moved to their new home, a mass of foreign ideas had come into their possession through contact with their new neighbors. While these new ideas were being reshaped and integrated, they stimulated the minds of the people, or of a few members of the tribe, who were thus led to the formation of a complex and detailed concept of the world. The concept which they have developed agrees in all its main features with those created by people of other zones and of other races. The mind of the Bella Coola philosopher, working with the kind of knowledge common to the earlier <term>strata of culture</term><note>Boas refers here to stages of social and intellectual development. He was a pioneer in arguing that all human minds operate with the same logic, regardless of their technology or social structure.</note>, has reached conclusions similar to those that have been formed by humans the world over, when working with that same kind of knowledge.

On the other hand, the Bella Coola has also adopted ready-made the thoughts of his neighbors, and has adapted them to his environment. These two results of our inquiry emphasize the close relation between the **comparative** and the **historical methods** of <term>ethnology</term><note>The study of the characteristics of various peoples and the differences and relationships between them; a precursor to modern cultural anthropology.</note>, which are so often held to be in opposition. Each is a check upon hasty conclusions that might be reached by applying only one. It is just as uncritical to see, in the similarity of a single cultural trait that occurs in two distinct regions, certain proof of an early historical connection as it is to reject the possibility of such a connection simply because the same ideas sometimes develop independently in the human mind. Ethnology is rapidly moving past the tendency to accept weak evidence as proof of historical connection; but the comparative ethnologist is only just beginning to see that he has no right to dismiss the historical method. Our inquiry shows that safe conclusions can be derived only by a careful analysis of the whole culture. The growth of the myths of the Bella Coola can be understood only when we consider the culture of the tribe as a whole. And so it is with other phenomena. All traits of culture can be fully under<unclear>sto</unclear>od only in connection with the whole culture of a tribe. When we limit ourselves to comparing isolated traits of culture, we open the door to countless misinterpretations.

If, then, the demand is made for a more critical method in the comparative study of ethnology than it has generally been granted, it does not imply a low opinion of the results of that method. When the human mind creates an idea, or when it borrows that same idea, we may assume that it has been created or accepted because it fits with the way the human mind is organized; otherwise it would not be created or accepted. The wider the distribution of an idea, whether original or borrowed, the closer must be its conformity with the laws governing the activities of the human mind. Historical analysis will provide the data regarding the growth of ideas among different people; and comparisons of the processes of their growth will give us knowledge of the laws which govern the evolution and selection of ideas.

<summary>Boas concludes that Nuxalk mythology is a unique local development that integrated foreign ideas into a sophisticated worldview. He argues that ethnology must combine historical study (where ideas come from) with comparative study (how the human mind works) to understand a culture in its entirety.</summary>
<keywords>Nuxalk, Bella Coola, ethnology, comparative method, historical method, cultural evolution, human psychology, methodology, Franz Boas, mythology</keywords>

<note>Fig. 9.—Mask representing <term>Snitsma'na</term> <note>A spirit associated with the sisaok' ceremonies</note>. Colors include the natural wood and black, with blue spots and red lines. Height: 9½ inches. Museum Catalogue No. 16/1419.</note>

<note>Fig. 10.—Mask representing <term>Aiatilā'axa</term> <note>A supernatural being that resides in the House of Myths</note> (shown in front and profile views). Colored in black, blue, and red. Height: 10½ inches. Museum Catalogue No. 16/1420.</note>

<note>Fig. 11.—Mask representing <term>AiaLilā'axa</term> <note>A variation or companion spirit to the Aiatilā'axa</note> (shown in front and profile views). Colored in red, green, and black. Height: 10 inches. Museum Catalogue No. 16/1421.</note>

<note>Fig. 12.—Mask representing <term>S'anōLx'mula'lt</term> <note>A figure representing a specific dancer or spirit from the secret society rituals</note>. Colors are the natural wood, black, and orange. Height: 9 inches. Museum Catalogue No. 16/1417.</note>

<note>Fig. 13.—Mask representing <term>S'anōLx'mula'lt</term> <note>A different version of the spirit described in Fig. 12</note>. Colors include blue, black, red, and the natural color of the wood. Height: 10 inches. Museum Catalogue No. 16/1418.</note>

<summary>This page lists and describes thirteen ceremonial masks of the Nuxalk people, detailing their physical dimensions, color schemes, and the mythological beings they represent, such as the Sun and the supreme deity.</summary>

# EXPLANATION OF PLATE IX.

-><note>*⁂ Cross-hatching (intersecting lines) indicates the color black; vertical hatching (up-and-down lines) indicates red; white represents the natural color of the wood.</note><-

<note>Fig. 1.—Mask representing the **Celestial Being** <note>original: "Xēmxēmalâ'gLla"; a prominent figure in the Nuxalk sky world, often associated with the sun or the supreme deity, Ātlquntām.</note> (shown from the front and in profile). Design: full moon. Natural wood color, black, red. Height, 13½ inches. Catalogue Number 16/1460.</note>

<note>Fig. 2.—Mask representing **He Who Comes from the Sun** <note>original: "Xē'mtsiwa"; a name associated with the solar lineage and high-ranking ancestral spirits.</note> (shown from the front and in profile). Design: full moon. Natural wood color, black, red. Height, 12 inches. Catalogue Number 16/1461.</note>

<note>Fig. 3.—Mask representing **The One in the Moon** <note>original: "Ōmq'ōmklī'lik'a"</note> (shown from the front and in profile). Design: half-moon. Natural wood color, black, red. Height, 12 inches. Catalogue Number 16/1462.</note>

<note>Fig. 4.—Mask representing the **Celestial Companion** <note>original: "Q'ō'mtsiwa"</note> (shown from the front and in profile). Design: half-moon. Natural wood color, black, red. Height, 12 inches. Catalogue Number 16/1463.</note>

<note>Fig. 5.—Mask representing **The Star-Bearer** <note>original: "Ai'umki'lik'a"; often interpreted as a spirit that carries the stars across the sky.</note> (shown from the front and in profile). Design: stars. Natural wood color, black, red. Height, 12 inches. Catalogue Number 16/1465.</note>

<note>Fig. 6.—Mask representing **He Who Wanders in the Woods** <note>original: "Kᵘlē'lias"</note>, wearing a ceremonial ring of red cedar-bark <note>Red cedar-bark rings are sacred regalia signifying that the wearer has been initiated into a secret society, such as the *Sisaok* or *Kusiut*.</note> (shown from the front and in profile). Design: rainbow. Natural wood color, black, red. Height, 14 inches. Catalogue Number 16/1466.</note>

<note>Fig. 7.—Mask representing the **Salmon-Berry Blossom** <note>original: "Q'ulaxā'wa"</note> (shown from the front and in profile). Design: salmon-berry blossom. Natural wood color, black, red. Height, 12 inches. Catalogue Number 16/1467.</note>

<note>Fig. 8.—Mask representing the **Kingfisher** <note>original: "Ā't'mākᵘ"</note>. The carving shows wings at the sides of the head and the bird's tail resting over the forehead. Natural wood color, black. Height, 12 inches. Catalogue Number 16/1468.</note>

<note>Fig. 9.—Mask representing **The One with the Grease** <note>original: "L'ētsā'aplēLāna"; the name refers to oolichan fish oil, a symbol of extreme wealth and abundance in Northwest Coast cultures.</note>, wearing a ceremonial ring of red and white cedar-bark (shown from the front and in profile). Design: grease-bladder <note>A traditional container made from animal tissue used to store and transport valuable oils.</note>. Natural wood color, black, red. Height, 14½ inches. Catalogue Number 16/1469.</note>

# EXPLANATION OF PLATE XI.

->\* \* <term>Cross-hatching</term> <note>original: "Cross-hachure"; a method of using fine parallel lines to indicate color or shading in a drawing</note> indicates black; vertical hatching, red; horizontal hatching, blue; dots, orange; white represents the natural color of wood, except in cases where the whole mask is black.<-

---

**Fig. 1.** — Mask representing the <term>thunder-bird</term> <note>A powerful, supernatural bird of prey from Pacific Northwest mythology that is said to cause thunder by flapping its wings and lightning by blinking its eyes</note> (shown in front and profile views). Black, red. Height, 11½ inches. Cat. No. 16/1443.

**Fig. 2.** — Mask representing <term>Alxulā'tenum</term> <note>A significant supernatural being in Nuxalk cosmology</note> (shown in front and profile views). Ornamented with red <term>cedar-bark</term> <note>The inner bark of the Western Red Cedar, often shredded and dyed red, is a primary material for sacred ritual regalia</note>. Orange, blue. Height, 12 inches. Cat. No. 16/1447.

**Fig. 3.** — Mask representing the rabbit. Set with mountain-goat skin. Natural color, black. Height, 13 inches. Cat. No. 16/1454.

**Fig. 4.** — Mask representing the owl. Black, red. Height, 12 inches. Cat. No. 16/1456.

**Fig. 5.** — Mask representing <term>Lō'qots</term> <note>original: "Lō'qots"; translates to "the mountain"</note> (the mountain). Natural color, black. Height, 19½ inches. Cat. No. 16/1448.

**Fig. 6.** — Mask representing the raindrop. On top of the mask is a long switch set with feathers, only the lower part of which is shown. Natural color, black. Height, 10 inches. Cat. No. 16/1450.

**Fig. 7.** — Bird-shaped implement worn by a companion of the <term>thunder-bird</term>. It is filled with <term>eagle-down</term> <note>Sacred in many Indigenous cultures, eagle down is released during dances to symbolize peace, blessing, and the presence of spirits</note>. During the dance it is shaken, and the down flies out of the holes in the lower part of the implement and from the back. Length, 18 inches. Cat. No. 16/1452.

**Fig. 8.** — Mask representing the sea-monster <term>K'î'lx'ta</term> <note>A legendary creature of the deep in Nuxalk oral tradition</note>. Red, blue, black. Height, 19 inches. Cat. No. 16/1502.

**Fig. 9.** — Mask representing the spirit <term>Lalaiā'iL</term> <note>A specific spiritual entity from the Nuxalk supernatural world</note>. Black, orange, set with bear-skin. Height, 9 inches. Cat. No. 16/1539.

**Fig. 10.** — Mask representing the <term>hermaphrodite</term> <note>A figure exhibiting both male and female characteristics, appearing in specific ceremonial contexts and creation myths</note> (shown in front and profile views). Natural color, red, black. Height, 11 inches. Cat. No. 16/1522.
